# Third-party notices

CanViewer is free software: you can redistribute it and/or modify it under the terms of
the GNU General Public License as published by the Free Software Foundation, either
version 2 of the License, or (at your option) any later version. [COPYRIGHT](COPYRIGHT)
holds the notice, with an additional permission for linking the Windows build with
Microsoft’s WebView2 loader and Visual C++ runtime; [LICENSE](LICENSE) holds the text of
version 2 of the GPL, and [LICENSE-GPL-3.0](LICENSE-GPL-3.0) the text of version 3.

CanViewer ships the third-party components below, each under its own license. Some are
under the Apache License 2.0, which is compatible with version 3 of the GNU GPL but not
with version 2. Where such a part is included (the Material Symbols icon paths in the web
app, and crates such as `tao` in the desktop app), the combined program is distributed
under the terms of the GNU GPL version 3.

Each component’s source is at the address given with it. The exact versions are pinned in
`package-lock.json`, `app/src/lib/canmatrix/wheels.lock.json` and `desktop/Cargo.lock`,
which are part of CanViewer’s source at every release tag.

This file is generated by `node scripts/third-party-notices.mjs`. To change it, change that
script or `scripts/license-policy.mjs`, not this file.

The wrappers and helpers with which Vite’s copy of the plugin bundles CommonJS modules such as React. The text is the plugin’s entry in Vite’s license file.

- License: MIT

### Pyodide runtime

In `pyodide/` next to the app (`pyodide.asm.mjs`, `pyodide.asm.wasm`, `python_stdlib.zip`), with the loader in the import worker’s JavaScript. It loads only when a file in a format other than DBC is opened. The parts after the `pyodide` package itself are compiled into it.

#### pyodide

- Version: 314.0.7
- License: MPL-2.0
- Source: <https://github.com/pyodide/pyodide/tree/314.0.7>
- License text: [Text 7](#text-7)

#### CPython

The interpreter in pyodide.asm.wasm and its standard library in python_stdlib.zip, with the code CPython incorporates that the second text lists (expat, libmpdec, mimalloc, SipHash24 and others). HACL* and KaRaMeL, which CPython also incorporates, are listed below. Some standard-library files carry their own notice, which python_stdlib.zip keeps in the file: tomllib (MIT), profile.py and pstats.py (Apache-2.0).

- Version: 3.14.2
- License: PSF-2.0
- Source: <https://github.com/python/cpython/tree/v3.14.2>
- License text: [Text 8](#text-8), [Text 9](#text-9)

#### HACL* (in CPython)

The hash functions of CPython’s hashlib, from CPython’s Modules/_hacl/ (the commit its refresh.sh records), compiled into pyodide.asm.wasm. The text is the header of those files.

- Version: commit 8ba599b
- License: MIT
- Source: <https://github.com/hacl-star/hacl-star/tree/8ba599b2f6c9701b3dc961db895b0856a2210f76>
- License text: [Text 10](#text-10)

#### KaRaMeL krmllib (in CPython’s HACL* copy)

The runtime headers of the KaRaMeL compiler that HACL* is generated with, compiled into pyodide.asm.wasm. Their files state the notice in the first text.

- License: Apache-2.0 AND MIT
- Source: <https://github.com/python/cpython/tree/v3.14.2/Modules/_hacl/include/krml>
- License text: [Text 11](#text-11), [Text 4](#text-4), [Text 12](#text-12)

#### GNU libiconv (compiled into lxml)

The character-set conversion that libxml2 uses, linked statically into lxml’s `etree` module (`lxml/etree.cpython-314-wasm32-emscripten.so`) by Pyodide’s build: pyodide-recipes 314-20260911 builds libiconv 1.16 as a static library (packages/libiconv) and links it into lxml 6.1.3 (packages/lxml, `-liconv`). The library is under the GNU Library General Public License, version 2 or (at your option) any later version. The lxml wheel is a file of its own in `pyodide/`, which can be replaced with one rebuilt from these sources.

- Version: 1.16
- License: LGPL-2.0-or-later
- Source: <https://ftp.gnu.org/gnu/libiconv/libiconv-1.16.tar.gz>, <https://github.com/pyodide/pyodide-recipes/tree/314-20260911/packages/libiconv>, <https://github.com/pyodide/pyodide-recipes/tree/314-20260911/packages/lxml>
- License text: [Text 36](#text-36)

### Windows installers

This section applies to the two Windows installers only, the `.msi` and the `-setup.exe`; no other package holds these parts. Tauri’s bundler builds the `.msi` with the WiX Toolset and the `-setup.exe` with NSIS, and each installer keeps parts of the tool that made it. These parts make up the installer program, which installs and removes CanViewer and comes in the same file; they are not part of the app. Neither holds the WebView2 installer: they download Microsoft’s bootstrapper at install time when WebView2 is missing.

#### WiX Toolset: WixUI and its custom actions

In the `.msi`: the dialogs of WixUI_InstallDir with their bitmaps, icons and strings (src/ext/UIExtension/wixlib), and uica.dll, the custom actions with which those dialogs check the chosen folder and print the license (src/ext/UIExtension/ca, linked with the libraries in src/libs/dutil and src/libs/wcautil). The source code of each of these files is in WiX’s v3.14.1 release (tag wix3141rtm), at the address below, under the Microsoft Reciprocal License (WiX’s LICENSE.TXT, the license text below).

- Version: 3.14.1
- License: MS-RL
- Source: <https://github.com/wixtoolset/wix3/tree/wix3141rtm>
- License text: [Text 243](#text-243)

#### NSIS

In the `-setup.exe`: the installer stub and the uninstaller it writes, the plugins the installer calls (System, nsDialogs, StartMenu and NSISdl), the Modern UI 2, the include headers (StrFunc among them), the language files and the default graphics. NSIS’s COPYING (the license text below) puts them under the zlib/libpng license. The address below is NSIS’s 3.13 source release; NSIS’s repository has the same source as the tag [v313](https://github.com/kichik/nsis/tree/v313).

- Version: 3.13
- License: Zlib
- Source: <https://sourceforge.net/projects/nsis/files/NSIS%203/3.13/nsis-3.13-src.tar.bz2>
- License text: [Text 244](#text-244)

#### NSIS LZMA compression module

In the `-setup.exe`: the LZMA decompressor in the installer stub, which the uninstaller shares, and which unpacks the installer’s data (Tauri compresses it with LZMA, NSIS’s default). It is under the Common Public License 1.0, with the special exception for the LZMA compression module at the end of NSIS’s COPYING. The source code of the LZMA compression module is available in the NSIS 3.13 source release, `nsis-3.13-src.tar.bz2`, at the address below, in its directory `Source/7zip/`.

- Version: 3.13
- License: CPL-1.0
- Source: <https://sourceforge.net/projects/nsis/files/NSIS%203/3.13/nsis-3.13-src.tar.bz2>
- License text: [Text 244](#text-244)

#### nsis_tauri_utils (NSIS plugin)

In the `-setup.exe`: `nsis_tauri_utils.dll`, Tauri’s plugin with which the installer compares versions, replaces text in strings and starts the app as the user, built from the crate crates/nsis-tauri-utils. The crates it links follow. Like any Rust library, it also holds parts of the Rust standard library (MIT OR Apache-2.0).

- Version: 0.5.3
- License: MIT OR Apache-2.0
- Source: <https://github.com/tauri-apps/nsis-tauri-utils/tree/nsis_tauri_utils-v0.5.3>
- License text: [Text 245](#text-245), [Text 4](#text-4)

A. HISTORY OF THE SOFTWARE
==========================

Python was created in the early 1990s by Guido van Rossum at Stichting
Mathematisch Centrum (CWI, see https://www.cwi.nl) in the Netherlands
as a successor of a language called ABC.  Guido remains Python's
principal author, although it includes many contributions from others.

In 1995, Guido continued his work on Python at the Corporation for
National Research Initiatives (CNRI, see https://www.cnri.reston.va.us)
in Reston, Virginia where he released several versions of the
software.

In May 2000, Guido and the Python core development team moved to
BeOpen.com to form the BeOpen PythonLabs team.  In October of the same
year, the PythonLabs team moved to Digital Creations, which became
Zope Corporation.  In 2001, the Python Software Foundation (PSF, see
https://www.python.org/psf/) was formed, a non-profit organization
created specifically to own Python-related Intellectual Property.
Zope Corporation was a sponsoring member of the PSF.

All Python releases are Open Source (see https://opensource.org for
the Open Source Definition).  Historically, most, but not all, Python
releases have also been GPL-compatible; the table below summarizes
the various releases.

    Release         Derived     Year        Owner       GPL-
                    from                                compatible? (1)

    0.9.0 thru 1.2              1991-1995   CWI         yes
    1.3 thru 1.5.2  1.2         1995-1999   CNRI        yes
    1.6             1.5.2       2000        CNRI        no
    2.0             1.6         2000        BeOpen.com  no
    1.6.1           1.6         2001        CNRI        yes (2)
    2.1             2.0+1.6.1   2001        PSF         no
    2.0.1           2.0+1.6.1   2001        PSF         yes
    2.1.1           2.1+2.0.1   2001        PSF         yes
    2.1.2           2.1.1       2002        PSF         yes
    2.1.3           2.1.2       2002        PSF         yes
    2.2 and above   2.1.1       2001-now    PSF         yes

Footnotes:

(1) GPL-compatible doesn't mean that we're distributing Python under
    the GPL.  All Python licenses, unlike the GPL, let you distribute
    a modified version without making your changes open source.  The
    GPL-compatible licenses make it possible to combine Python with
    other software that is released under the GPL; the others don't.

(2) According to Richard Stallman, 1.6.1 is not GPL-compatible,
    because its license has a choice of law clause.  According to
    CNRI, however, Stallman's lawyer has told CNRI's lawyer that 1.6.1
    is "not incompatible" with the GPL.

Thanks to the many outside volunteers who have worked under Guido's
direction to make these releases possible.

SipHash24
---------

The file :file:`Python/pyhash.c` contains Marek Majkowski' implementation of
Dan Bernstein's SipHash24 algorithm. It contains the following note::

strtod and dtoa
---------------

The file :file:`Python/dtoa.c`, which supplies C functions dtoa and
strtod for conversion of C doubles to and from strings, is derived
from the file of the same name by David M. Gay, currently available
from https://web.archive.org/web/20220517033456/http://www.netlib.org/fp/dtoa.c.

OpenSSL
-------

the OpenSSL library for added performance if made available by the
operating system. Additionally, the Windows and macOS installers for

of the OpenSSL license here. For the OpenSSL 3.0 release,

et_xml is licensed under the MIT license; see the file LICENCE for details.

et_xml includes code from the Python standard library, which is licensed under
the Python license, a permissive open source license. The copyright and license
is included below for compliance with Python's terms.

This module includes corrections and new features as follows:
- Correct handling of attributes namespaces when a default namespace
  has been registered.
- Records the namespaces for an Element during parsing and utilises them to
  allow inspection of namespaces at specific elements in the xml tree and
  during serialisation.

Misc:
- Includes the test_xml_etree with small modifications for testing the
  modifications in this package.

A. HISTORY OF THE SOFTWARE
==========================

Python was created in the early 1990s by Guido van Rossum at Stichting
Mathematisch Centrum (CWI, see https://www.cwi.nl) in the Netherlands
as a successor of a language called ABC.  Guido remains Python's
principal author, although it includes many contributions from others.

In 1995, Guido continued his work on Python at the Corporation for
National Research Initiatives (CNRI, see https://www.cnri.reston.va.us)
in Reston, Virginia where he released several versions of the
software.

In May 2000, Guido and the Python core development team moved to
BeOpen.com to form the BeOpen PythonLabs team.  In October of the same
year, the PythonLabs team moved to Digital Creations, which became
Zope Corporation.  In 2001, the Python Software Foundation (PSF, see
https://www.python.org/psf/) was formed, a non-profit organization
created specifically to own Python-related Intellectual Property.
Zope Corporation was a sponsoring member of the PSF.

All Python releases are Open Source (see https://opensource.org for
the Open Source Definition).  Historically, most, but not all, Python
releases have also been GPL-compatible; the table below summarizes
the various releases.

    Release         Derived     Year        Owner       GPL-
                    from                                compatible? (1)

    0.9.0 thru 1.2              1991-1995   CWI         yes
    1.3 thru 1.5.2  1.2         1995-1999   CNRI        yes
    1.6             1.5.2       2000        CNRI        no
    2.0             1.6         2000        BeOpen.com  no
    1.6.1           1.6         2001        CNRI        yes (2)
    2.1             2.0+1.6.1   2001        PSF         no
    2.0.1           2.0+1.6.1   2001        PSF         yes
    2.1.1           2.1+2.0.1   2001        PSF         yes
    2.1.2           2.1.1       2002        PSF         yes
    2.1.3           2.1.2       2002        PSF         yes
    2.2 and above   2.1.1       2001-now    PSF         yes

Footnotes:

(1) GPL-compatible doesn't mean that we're distributing Python under
    the GPL.  All Python licenses, unlike the GPL, let you distribute
    a modified version without making your changes open source.  The
    GPL-compatible licenses make it possible to combine Python with
    other software that is released under the GPL; the others don't.

(2) According to Richard Stallman, 1.6.1 is not GPL-compatible,
    because its license has a choice of law clause.  According to
    CNRI, however, Stallman's lawyer has told CNRI's lawyer that 1.6.1
    is "not incompatible" with the GPL.

Thanks to the many outside volunteers who have worked under Guido's
direction to make these releases possible.
